If there’s some deeper reason, I’m not sure I’d know how to tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating episodes are in any way related to sexual feelings or guilt about sexual activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. I mean, I can’t say it’s impossible in some deep way, but when it happens, I’m not sitting there thinking about sex or feeling guilty about sexual things.\n\nUsually it’s much more ordinary in my head—like what’s in the cupboard, or whether I should’ve called someone back, or some nonsense from the day. Then I’ll remember I forgot to take the chicken out to thaw, and next thing you know I’m talking about the freezer instead of what you asked me.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a connection like that. If there is one, I don’t really recognize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sexually satisfied in your relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. My husband and I have been together a long time, and things change, you know. It’s not like when you’re younger and everything feels urgent.\n\n“Sexually satisfied” sounds like something I’d have to sit and think about, and then I start thinking about whether we ever replaced that old mattress, because my back hurts sometimes, and then I’m off thinking about sheets and laundry.\n\nI guess I wouldn’t say it’s something I spend a lot of time thinking about. I don’t know if that answers you very well.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your eating episodes are a way to avoid thinking about your own sexual desires or feelings?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.

When I’m eating, it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to avoid sexual feelings, exactly. It feels more like I’m just eating and then I look up and realize I’ve gone too far. My mind is usually on silly things, like whether I need to buy paper towels, or if I paid the electric bill, and then I’ll remember something from years ago that has nothing to do with anything.

So… I can’t say yes to that. Maybe there are feelings under it I’m not noticing, but I don’t really recognize it as being about sexual desires.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came because after these nights where I eat, I end up just sitting there with crumbs and empty containers all around me, and I feel so ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s hard to even pray after, and then the next morning at church I can hardly look people in the eye. I keep thinking they can somehow see it on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating because you're hungry during these nights?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if I’d call it hungry, exactly. Sometimes I start thinking maybe I just skipped something earlier, but then… I don’t know, hunger feels like when my stomach’s growling, you know? These nights feel different, but it’s hard for me to explain. My sister used to say you should never eat after supper because it “sits on you,” and I think about that too sometimes. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on these nights?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel like I can. Once I take that first bite, especially after the house is quiet, it’s like my good sense just steps aside. I’ll be saying to myself, “Belinda, stop now, that’s enough,” but my hands just keep going back for more. It’s like I’m watching myself do it and still not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… terribly guilty. After those nights, I’ll sit there and see the crumbs and empty containers around me, and I just feel so ashamed. Disgusted with myself, honestly. And then I start thinking about how I was raised, how my mother used to say you should treat your body with respect, and—well, the next morning at church I can hardly look folks in the eye. I feel like they can somehow tell, even though I know that doesn’t make much sense. It makes it hard to even pray sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sexually aroused when you're eating like this at night?\n\nPatient: Sexually aroused? No… I don’t think so. That’s not really what it feels like to me. It’s more like I’m just caught up in the eating, and then afterward I feel awful about it. I mean, I remember my aunt used to talk about certain foods being “too rich” like they could stir up all kinds of things, but… no, I haven’t noticed it being sexual, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed or anxious before these nights when you eat like this?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure, doctor. I haven’t kept track of whether I’m anxious before it happens. Sometimes the house gets quiet and I’m just there with my thoughts, you know, and then one thing leads to another. But I couldn’t honestly say, “Yes, it’s always stress,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s funny, my sister used to say I worried too much about everything, even little things like whether the curtains matched the sofa, and I suppose maybe I do get wound up about things, but before those nights specifically… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're doing when you're eating like this at night?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t feel in control, not really. Once the house is quiet and I take that first bite, it’s like my good sense just steps aside somewhere. I’ll be telling myself, “Belinda, stop now, that’s enough,” but my hands just keep reaching for more anyway.\n\nIt’s strange, because in other things I can be very particular—my pantry shelves, for instance, I like the cans facing the same way—but with this, at night, it just… gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest in sex around the same time these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything like that, doctor. I mean, at my age, it’s not exactly something I’m sitting around measuring, if you know what I mean. My husband used to joke that I’d rather rearrange the linen closet than talk about romance, but that was just his teasing.\n\nAround the time the eating started… I can’t really say there was some clear change in that department. It’s hard for me to connect those two things. I haven’t paid much attention to it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about sex or have sexual thoughts during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: Sexual thoughts? No… I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway. When I’m eating at night, I’m mostly just sort of caught up in the food and the kitchen being quiet, and then I’ll notice crumbs on the counter or think I should’ve wiped the stovetop earlier.\n\nI don’t really remember thinking about sex during it. It’s not the sort of thing that comes to mind then, unless I’m just not paying attention to my own thoughts, which I suppose is possible. My mind does wander, but not really in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Do you use eating as a way to distract yourself from sexual thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no. I mean, I can’t say I’ve noticed it working that way. When I go to the kitchen, it’s more like—well, the house is quiet, and I’ll see something sitting there, or remember there’s pie left, and then one thing leads to another.\n\nSexual feelings aren’t really what I’m trying to get away from, as far as I can tell. Though now you ask, it makes me think of how my mother never liked anyone talking about those things at the table—she’d clear her throat and change the subject to the neighbors’ hedges. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut no, I don’t believe I’m eating to distract myself from that. At least, not in any way I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be related to feelings of guilt about sex or sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor. I don’t really think of it that way. Guilt about sex… that sounds like something I’d have to sit with a minute.\n\nI mean, I was raised not to talk about those things much, and even saying “sexual activity” out loud feels a little like when my aunt used to whisper certain words like the wallpaper could hear her. But when I’m eating, I can’t say I’m thinking, “Oh, I feel guilty about that,” and then going to the kitchen.\n\nIt feels more separate than that to me. At least as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed a connection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be a way of coping with uncomfortable feelings or thoughts related to sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, uncomfortable feelings, sure, people have them, don’t they—but related to sex specifically… I haven’t noticed that being part of it.\n\nWhen it happens, I’m usually not thinking in that direction. It’s more like the house is quiet and I’m in the kitchen, and then I remember what’s in the cupboard. Though kitchens are funny places, aren’t they? You can remember every little thing your mother said standing by the sink, even if it was fifty years ago.\n\nBut no, I don’t have a clear sense that I’m eating because of thoughts or feelings about sex. If that’s there, I’m not aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be a way to avoid thinking about sex or sexual feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any clear way. I mean, I can’t say I sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about sex,” and then reach for food.\n\nMostly it’s more muddled than that. I’ll be in the kitchen, or passing through, and then I notice something, and suddenly I’m eating. It doesn’t feel connected to sexual feelings, at least not that I can put my finger on.\n\nThough it’s funny, the things we avoid thinking about—you don’t always know you’re avoiding them, do you? My mother used to say, “Some subjects are private,” like that settled everything. But as for the eating, no… I haven’t noticed that being the reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed any pattern between your eating episodes and when you've been intimate with your husband?\n\nPatient: I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern, no. I mean… with my husband, after all these years, things are not exactly like they were when we were young, if you know what I mean. Not that I need to go into all that.\n\nBut eating afterward, or before, or because of it… I don’t think I’ve made that connection. Sometimes I eat when I’m alone in the kitchen, sometimes it’s after a long day, sometimes I’m just restless. And then I start thinking about whether I took something out of the freezer for tomorrow, or whether the towels got moved again—little things like that.\n\nSo no, not really. I haven’t noticed intimacy with my husband being tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be a way to deal with feelings of guilt or discomfort related to your sexual relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Guilt or discomfort about that part of my marriage… I mean, every marriage has its awkward bits, doesn’t it? Things you don’t talk about at the breakfast table.\n\nBut when the eating happens, I don’t usually think, “Oh, this is because of my husband,” or because of anything between us. It’s more like I’m already in the kitchen and one thing leads to another. Sometimes I’m thinking about whether I paid the electric bill, or whether the neighbor’s dog has been barking again—silly things.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed it being about guilt or discomfort in my sexual relationship. If there’s something underneath it, I can’t say I’ve connected those dots.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by thoughts or feelings related to your own sexuality?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. “My own sexuality” feels like a big phrase, and I’m not always sure what people mean by it.\n\nI mean, I’m 65 now, so you have thoughts about your body changing, and what life used to be like, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I bought the right kind of bread or if the mail came late again. My mind does that—it wanders off.\n\nBut as far as the eating goes, I haven’t sat there thinking, “This is because of sexual feelings,” or anything like that. If it’s connected somehow underneath, I wouldn’t know how to point to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be a way of punishing yourself for past sexual experiences or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. “Punishing myself” sounds so strong, and I can’t say I’ve ever thought of it that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve made plenty of mistakes in my life, like anyone, and sometimes you look back on things and think, “Why did I do that?” But then my mind jumps to something else—laundry, groceries, whether I left the porch light on. It’s hard for me to stay with one thought sometimes.\n\nWhen I’m eating like that, I don’t usually feel like I’m thinking about past sexual experiences or trying to punish myself for anything. It’s more just… I start eating, and then it gets away from me. If there’s some deeper reason, I’m not sure I’d know how to tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating episodes are in any way related to sexual feelings or guilt about sexual activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. When I go to the kitchen, it’s more like—well, the house is quiet, and I’ll see something sitting there, or remember there’s pie left, and then one thing leads to another.\n\nSexual feelings aren’t really what I’m trying to get away from, as far as I can tell. Though now you ask, it makes me think of how my mother never liked anyone talking about those things at the table—she’d clear her throat and change the subject to the neighbors’ hedges. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut no, I don’t believe I’m eating to distract myself from that. At least, not in any way I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be related to feelings of guilt about sex or sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor. I don’t really think of it that way. Guilt about sex… that sounds like something I’d have to sit with a minute.\n\nI mean, I was raised not to talk about those things much, and even saying “sexual activity” out loud feels a little like when my aunt used to whisper certain words like the wallpaper could hear her. But when I’m eating, I can’t say I’m thinking, “Oh, I feel guilty about that,” and then going to the kitchen.\n\nIt feels more separate than that to me. At least as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed a connection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be a way of coping with uncomfortable feelings or thoughts related to sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, uncomfortable feelings, sure, people have them, don’t they—but related to sex specifically… I haven’t noticed that being part of it.\n\nWhen it happens, I’m usually not thinking in that direction. It’s more like the house is quiet and I’m in the kitchen, and then I remember what’s in the cupboard. Though kitchens are funny places, aren’t they? You can remember every little thing your mother said standing by the sink, even if it was fifty years ago.\n\nBut no, I don’t have a clear sense that I’m eating because of thoughts or feelings about sex. If that’s there, I’m not aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be a way to avoid thinking about sex or sexual feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any clear way. I mean, I can’t say I sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about sex,” and then reach for food.\n\nMostly it’s more muddled than that. I’ll be in the kitchen, or passing through, and then I notice something, and suddenly I’m eating. It doesn’t feel connected to sexual feelings, at least not that I can put my finger on.\n\nThough it’s funny, the things we avoid thinking about—you don’t always know you’re avoiding them, do you? My mother used to say, “Some subjects are private,” like that settled everything. But as for the eating, no… I haven’t noticed that being the reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed any pattern between your eating episodes and when you've been intimate with your husband?\n\nPatient: I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern, no. I mean… with my husband, after all these years, things are not exactly like they were when we were young, if you know what I mean. Not that I need to go into all that.\n\nBut eating afterward, or before, or because of it… I don’t think I’ve made that connection. Sometimes I eat when I’m alone in the kitchen, sometimes it’s after a long day, sometimes I’m just restless. And then I start thinking about whether I took something out of the freezer for tomorrow, or whether the towels got moved again—little things like that.\n\nSo no, not really. I haven’t noticed intimacy with my husband being tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be a way to deal with feelings of guilt or discomfort related to your sexual relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Guilt or discomfort about that part of my marriage… I mean, every marriage has its awkward bits, doesn’t it? Things you don’t talk about at the breakfast table.\n\nBut when the eating happens, I don’t usually think, “Oh, this is because of my husband,” or because of anything between us. It’s more like I’m already in the kitchen and one thing leads to another. Sometimes I’m thinking about whether I paid the electric bill, or whether the neighbor’s dog has been barking again—silly things.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed it being about guilt or discomfort in my sexual relationship. If there’s something underneath it, I can’t say I’ve connected those dots.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by thoughts or feelings related to your own sexuality?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. “My own sexuality” feels like a big phrase, and I’m not always sure what people mean by it.\n\nI mean, I’m 65 now, so you have thoughts about your body changing, and what life used to be like, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I bought the right kind of bread or if the mail came late again. My mind does that—it wanders off.\n\nBut as far as the eating goes, I haven’t sat there thinking, “This is because of sexual feelings,” or anything like that. If it’s connected somehow underneath, I wouldn’t know how to point to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes might be a way of punishing yourself for past sexual experiences or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. “Punishing myself” sounds so strong, and I can’t say I’ve ever thought of it that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve made plenty of mistakes in my life, like anyone, and sometimes you look back on things and think, “Why did I do that?” But then my mind jumps to something else—laundry, groceries, whether I left the porch light on. It’s hard for me to stay with one thought sometimes.\n\nWhen I’m eating like that, I don’t usually feel like I’m thinking about past sexual experiences or trying to punish myself for anything. It’s more just… I start eating, and then it gets away from me. If there’s some deeper reason, I’m not sure I’d know how to tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating episodes are in any way related to sexual feelings or guilt about sexual activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. I mean, I can’t say it’s impossible in some deep way, but when it happens, I’m not sitting there thinking about sex or feeling guilty about sexual things.\n\nUsually it’s much more ordinary in my head—like what’s in the cupboard, or whether I should’ve called someone back, or some nonsense from the day. Then I’ll remember I forgot to take the chicken out to thaw, and next thing you know I’m talking about the freezer instead of what you asked me.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a connection like that. If there is one, I don’t really recognize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sexually satisfied in your relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. My husband and I have been together a long time, and things change, you know. It’s not like when you’re younger and everything feels urgent.\n\n“Sexually satisfied” sounds like something I’d have to sit and think about, and then I start thinking about whether we ever replaced that old mattress, because my back hurts sometimes, and then I’m off thinking about sheets and laundry.\n\nI guess I wouldn’t say it’s something I spend a lot of time thinking about. I don’t know if that answers you very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating episodes are a way to avoid thinking about your own sexual desires or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nWhen I’m eating, it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to avoid sexual feelings, exactly. It feels more like I’m just eating and then I look up and realize I’ve gone too far. My mind is usually on silly things, like whether I need to buy paper towels, or if I paid the electric bill, and then I’ll remember something from years ago that has nothing to do with anything.\n\nSo… I can’t say yes to that. Maybe there are feelings under it I’m not noticing, but I don’t really recognize it as being about sexual desires."
